                                    Retry
                                    ↓
                                    ❌
                                    ↓
                                    DLQ
                            The DLQ is useful because you don't simply lose the failed event.
                            You can later investigate/process it.
            Deployment:
                Deployment Size
                    If you're uploading a Lambda function as a ZIP:
                    ZIP compressed size: 50 MB
                    after extracting, Uncompressed size: 250 MB
        Cold Start:
            Lambda needs to prepare a new execution environment before running your function.
            Before run your function,
                AWS creates an execution environment for your function.
                    Lambda Function
                        │
                        ↓
                    Execution Environment
                        │
                        ├── Runtime
                        ├── Your code
                        ├── Dependencies
                        └── Initialization
                if execution environment already exists,
                    Warm Start:
                        very little setup is required.
                        Low latency.
                        flow:
                            Request
                            ↓
                            Existing Lambda environment
                            ↓
                            Handler
                            ↓
                            Response
                    Cold Start:
                        if not have a ready execution environment.
                        higher latency.
                        flow:
                            Request
                            ↓
                            Create new execution environment
                            ↓
                            Load runtime
                            ↓
                            Load your code
                            ↓
                            Load dependencies
                            ↓
                            Run initialization
                            ↓
                            Handler
                            ↓
                            Response
                        That preparation takes additional time.
                    Why doesn't every request cause a Cold Start?
                        lambda get request:
                            Request 1
                            ↓
                            Cold Start
                            ↓
                            Environment A

                            Request 2
                            ↓
                            Environment A
                            ↓
                            Handler
                        No new initialization is required.
                        That's a warm invocation.
                            Request 1 → 🥶 Cold
                            Request 2 → 🔥 Warm
                            Request 3 → 🔥 Warm
                            Request 4 → 🔥 Warm
                        However, Lambda execution environments aren't permanent. AWS may eventually remove an idle environment.
                        Then:
                            Request 5
                            ↓
                            New environment
                            ↓
                            🥶 Cold Start
                        Sudden traffic spike can cause:
                            Cold-start latency
        Provisioned Concurrency:
            prepare those environments in advance so the first request doesn't have to wait.
            AWS prepares environments before requests arrive.
            So the request doesn't need to wait for the initialization phase.
            use cases
                latency-sensitive APIs
                interactive applications
                user-facing applications
                workloads where predictable response time matters
            can scale with schedule scaling, target utilization.
            avoid cold-start latency
        Lambda SnapStart:
            improve functions performance to 10x with no extra costs.
            take a snapshot of an initialized environment and restore from that snapshot.
            reduce cold-start latency
            When you publish a new version:
                SnapStart prepares the snapshot for that published version.
                Imagine:
                    $LATEST
                    ↓
                    Publish
                    ↓
                    Version 5
                flow:
                    Lambda initializes your function
                    Takes a snapshot of memory and disk state of the initialized function
                    Snapshot is cached for low-latency access
            
            |                           | Provisioned Concurrency | SnapStart                         |
            | ------------------------- | ----------------------- | --------------------------------- |
            | Main goal                 | Avoid cold starts       | Reduce cold-start time            |
            | Prepares environment?     | ✅ Yes                   | ✅ During publish                  |
            | Keeps environments ready? | ✅ Yes                   | ❌ Not the same mechanism          |
            | Uses snapshots?           | ❌                       | ✅                                 |
            | Extra cost                | 💰 Yes                  | 🆓 No additional SnapStart charge |
            | Best idea                 | Predictable low latency | Faster startup                    |
        exam summary:
            | Limit                      |                          Remember |
            | -------------------------- | --------------------------------: |
            | **Maximum execution time** |                  ⭐ **15 minutes** |
            | **Memory**                 |              ⭐ **128 MB – 10 GB** |
            | **`/tmp` storage**         |              ⭐ **512 MB – 10 GB** |
            | **Concurrency**            | ⭐ **1,000 default, can increase** |

            | Limit                 |   Remember |
            | --------------------- | ---------: |
            | ZIP deployment        |  **50 MB** |
            | Uncompressed package  | **250 MB** |
            | Environment variables |   **4 KB** |

            Cold Start → new execution environment → initialization → higher first-request latency
            Provisioned Concurrency → pre-initialized environments → avoid cold-start latency
            Reserved Concurrency → maximum concurrency / protect function and account capacity

            my my Lab, AWS Lambda's Concurrent executions is 10 count(account-level quota value) only have. 
            to increase: AWS Console → Service Quotas → AWS Lambda -> search for: Concurrent executions
        Customize At the Edge:
            Normally, your application logic runs in a server/Region.
            Edge Functions let you run small pieces of code close to the user, at CloudFront edge locations.
            Edge Function:
                Code that runs at/near a CloudFront edge location.
                minimize latency.
            two different edge-computing:
                CloudFront Functions:
                    very lightweight, high-scale operations. 
                    Lambda functions must be written by JavaScript.
                    Small + fast + simple
                    Millions of requests per second.
                    Used to change Viewer requests and responses:
                        Viewer Request: after CloudFront receives a request from a viewer.
                        Viewer Response: before CloudFront forwards the response to viewer.
                    Native feature of CloudFront(manage code entirely withing CloudFront)
                        Native = built directly into the CloudFront service.
                    Typical examples:
                        URL rewrites
                        URL redirects
                        Modify headers
                        Normalize requests
                        Cache-key manipulation
                        Simple authentication/authorization logic
                    flow:
                        User
                        ↓
                        CloudFront Edge
                        ↓
                        CloudFront Function
                        ↓
                        Very small/simple logic
                        ↓
                        Response / continue request
                Lambda@Edge:
                    More powerful logic at the edge
                    Lambda functions must be written in Node.js / Python
                    Thousands of requests per second.
                    Used to change CloudFront requests and responses:
                        Viewer Request - after CloudFront receives a request from a viewer
                        Origin Request - before CloudFront forwards the request to the ongin
                        Origin Response - after CloudFront receives the response from the origin
                        Viewer Response - before CloudFront forwards the response to the viewer

            Differences:
                |                   | CloudFront Functions                 | Lambda@Edge                                |
                | ----------------- | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ |
                | Purpose           | Lightweight edge logic               | More advanced edge logic                   |
                | Complexity        | Simple                               | More complex                               |
                | Startup           | Extremely fast                       | More capability, potentially more overhead |
                | Runtime           | JavaScript                           | Node.js / Python                           |
                | Execution time    | Very short                           | Longer than CloudFront Functions           |
                | Use case          | Headers, redirects, URL manipulation | Complex request/response processing        |
                | Runs at           | CloudFront edge                      | CloudFront edge                            |
                | Server management | ❌                                    | ❌                                          |

                | Feature         | CloudFront Functions | Lambda@Edge             |
                | --------------- | -------------------- | ----------------------- |
                | Main idea       | **Simple + fast**    | **Powerful + flexible** |
                | Runtime         | JavaScript           | Node.js / Python        |
                | Requests        | Millions/sec         | Thousands/sec           |
                | Viewer Request  | ✅                    | ✅                       |
                | Viewer Response | ✅                    | ✅                       |
                | Origin Request  | ❌                    | ✅                       |
                | Origin Response | ❌                    | ✅                       |
                | Execution       | **< 1 ms**           | **seconds**             |
                | Memory          | **2 MB**             | Much larger             |
                | Request body    | ❌                    | ✅                       |
                | Network access  | ❌                    | More capable            |
                | File system     | ❌                    | More capable            |
                | Package         | Very small           | Larger                  |
                | Cost            | Cheaper              | More expensive          |

            To customize requests/responses at CloudFront Edge Locations.
            Lambda@Edge functions must be deployed/created in Region: us-east-1.
                CloudFront distributes/replicates the function to its edge locations.
                                us-east-1
                                │
                                │ CloudFront replicates
                                ↓
                    ┌─────────┼─────────┐
                    ↓         ↓         ↓
                    🇯🇵        🇺🇸        🇬🇧
                    Edge       Edge       Edge
            Lambda in VPC:
                By default: Lambda is not automatically inside your VPC.
                Lambda is outside your VPC by default.
                    Your AWS Account
                        │
                        ├── Your VPC
                        │    ├── EC2
                        │    ├── RDS
                        │    └── ElastiCache
                        │
                        └── Lambda
                            └── AWS-managed environment
                So, Lambda cannot directly access private resources in your VPC.
                How do we put Lambda into the VPC?
                    When configuring Lambda, we need to specify:
                            ├── VPC ID
                            ├── Subnets
                            └── Security Groups
                    Lambda will create an ENI in your subnets
                        ENI = Elastic Network Interface
                        Think of an ENI as a virtual network interface/card.
                    This gives Lambda network connectivity to your VPC.
            Lambda + RDS Proxy:
                For Lambda → RDS direction.
                Lambda can create too many database connections under high concurrency.
                Although Lambda can scale extremely quickly, your database may not scale its connections at the same rate.
                RDS Proxy solves this.
                RDS Proxy sits between Lambda and RDS:
                        Lambda
                        Lambda
                        Lambda
                        Lambda
                        │
                        ↓
                        ┌──────────────┐
                        │  RDS Proxy   │
                        │              │
                        │ Connection   │
                        │    Pool      │
                        └──────┬───────┘
                            ↓
                            RDS
                The proxy pools and shares database connections.
                    connection pooling: a collection of reusable database connections,
                        instead of creating a new database connection for every request.
                RDS Proxy = connection pooling for your database.
                reducing failover time and preserving connections
                    Without a proxy, many Lambda/database connections may need to be recreated.
                integrates with: AWS Secrets Manager
                    enforcing IAM authentication and storing credentials in Secrets Manager
                Important:
                    RDS Proxy is never publicly accessible.
                    Lambda must be deployed in your VPC. and then it can access to RDS Proxy.
            Lambda + RDS/Aurora integration:
                RDS/Aurora can also trigger a Lambda function directly.
                database becomes the caller.
                support databases: PostgreSQL and Aurora MySQL.
                requirements:
                    Network connectivity:
                        Must allow outbound traffic to your Lambda function from within your DB instance.
                        RDS instance needs a way to reach Lambda:
                            Public connectivity
                            NAT Gateway
                            VPC Endpoint
                    Need permission on IAM Policy, Resource-based Policy.
                        Even if RDS can reach Lambda through the network, AWS still asks:
                            "Is this database actually allowed to invoke this Lambda?"
                        So, need to add permission. Think:
                            IAM Policy
                                → "RDS can invoke Lambda"
                            Lambda Resource Policy
                                → "I allow RDS to invoke me"
                RDS Event Notification:
                    tell you about changes/status of the RDS resource itself, NOT changes to the data inside the database.
                    tell you: "Something happened to my RDS infrastructure/resource."
                    not: It does NOT tell you about database data.
                    example:
                        DB instance created
                        DB instance started
                        DB instance stopped
                        DB instance rebooted
                    Near real-time events(up to 5 mintes)
                    Send notifications to SNS or subscribe to events using EventBridge.
    Amazon DynamoDB:
        fully managed, serverless NoSQL database
        Highly available + multiple AZs
        very high-scale, low-latency applications.
        unlike SQL database, stores items with attributes.
            attributes don't necessarily have to be identical for every item.
            that's one reason DynamoDB is called NoSQL.
        NoSQL — but supports transactions
        IAM integration
        Low cost + auto-scaling
            capacity modes
                Provisioned capacity(default):
                    can set reads/writes per seconds.
                    need to plan capacity beforehand.
                    pay for provisioned Read Capacity Units(RCU) and Write Capacity Units(WCU)
                    possibility to add auto-scaling mode RCU & WCU.
                On-demand capacity:
                    scale up/down read/writes automatically
                    no capacity planning.
                    pay for use, more expensive.
                    great for Unpredictable workloads, steep sudden spikes.
        has different table classes
            Standard => Frequent access
            Standard-IA => Infrequent access
        Basic:
            DynamoDB is made of Tables
            Each table stores a collection of items.
                Maximum item size = 400 KB
            Table → Item → Attribute
                | SQL    | DynamoDB      |
                | ------ | ------------- |
                | Table  | Table         |
                | Row    | **Item**      |
                | Column | **Attribute** |
                Attributes don't have to be identical.
                    This is called a flexible schema.
            Every DynamoDB table must have a Primary Key.
                Primary Key must be decided when the table is created.
            Partition Key:
                an attribute used to identify and distribute items.
            Sort Key:
                second key that can be used together with the Partition Key.
            Simple Primary Key:
                Simple Primary Key contains only a Partition Key.
            Composite Primary Key:
                Partition Key + Sort Key
        Data Types:
            Scalar Types
                Scalar means basically one value.
            Document Types
                List,Map to allow you to create nested structures.
                List => array
                Map => Object / Dictionary.
            Set Types
                collection of unique values of the same type.
                    String Set
                    Number Set
                    Binary Set
        can repidly evolve schemas:
            DynamoDB has a flexible schema at the item/attribute level.
                but, Primary Key is NOT flexible.
        DynamoDB Accerlerator(DAX):
            Sits in front of DynamoDB to cache frequently read data.
            flow: Application → DAX → DynamoDB
            Helps reduce read congestion on DynamoDB.
            Cached data can be returned in microseconds.
            No application code changes required because DAX is compatible with existing DynamoDB APIs.
            Default TTL = 5 minutes.
            DAX vs ElastiCache:
                DAX → specifically designed for DynamoDB, minimal/no application changes.
                ElastiCache → general-purpose Redis/Memcached cache;
                    usually requires application-level caching logic.
        Stream Processing:
            DynamoDB Streams / Kinesis Data Streams can capture item-level changes in DynamoDB.
            stream:
                A record of changes happening to items in your DynamoDB table.
                flow:
                    DynamoDB
                        │
                        │ Item changed
                        ↓
                    DynamoDB Streams
                        ↓
                    AWS Lambda
                        ↓
                    Process change
            react to changes in real-time
            Insert into derivative tables
                Derivative = derived from something else
                Whenever the original data changes, Lambda can update the derived table.
            Cross-region replication
            two type of straming:
                DynamoDB Streams:
                    24 hours Rentention.
                    limited no of consumes.
                    Lambda triggers
                        New item
                        ↓
                        DynamoDB
                        ↓
                        Stream
                        ↓
                        Lambda automatically invoked
                        ↓
                        Send notification
                    can be consumed by Lambda or through the DynamoDB Streams Kinesis Adapter.
                Kinesis Data Streams:
                    1 year Rentention
                    high no of consumers.
                    using AWS lambda, Kinesis Data Analytics, etc.
            DynamoDB Streams vs Kinesis Data Streams
                |                             | DynamoDB Streams              | Kinesis Data Streams          |
                | --------------------------- | ----------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
                | Purpose                     | Capture DynamoDB item changes | General-purpose streaming     |
                | Retention                   | **24 hours**                  | **Up to 365 days**            |
                | Consumers                   | More limited                  | **Many consumers**            |
                | Replay                      | Limited to retention window   | Much better replay capability |
                | Lambda                      | ✅                             | ✅                             |
                | Firehose integration        | ❌ directly                    | ✅                             |
                | General streaming ecosystem | Limited                       | **Excellent**                 |
            Stream Vs DAX:
                DAX:
                    speed up reads
                    Caching → improve read performance
                DynamoDB Streams:
                    detect item changes
                    Capture changes → react to events
            DynamoDB Global Tables:
                multi-Region DynamoDB
                Active-Active replication
                Applications can READ and WRITE in any Region
                    major difference of one Region is primary and another is standby.
                Must enable: DynamoDB Streams required
                Global Tables vs Multi-AZ:
                    Multi-AZ → availability
                    Global Tables → multi-Region + low latency + disaster recovery, Active-Active
                        (Multiple Regions can actively handle reads and writes.)
                    RDS Multi-AZ -> One primary handles writes; standby is for failover.
            TTL(Time To Live):
                automatically deletes expired items
                TTL is NOT exactly "delete at this second"
                TTL deletion is asynchronous.
                Don't use TTL when you need something to be deleted at an exact second.
                useful:
                    Session data.
                        User login session
                            ↓
                        expires after 24 hours
                            ↓
                        TTL automatically removes it
                    Temporary data:
                        Temporary verification code
                            ↓
                        expires after 10 minutes
                            ↓
                        TTL removes it
                    Logs / events:
                        Event
                        ↓
                        30 days
                        ↓
                        TTL
                        ↓
                        Automatically deleted
                TTL saves you from manually deleting items
                    a server-side automatic cleanup mechanism.
            Backups for disaster recovery:
                two types:
                    PITR(point-in-time recovery):
                        continuously back up your DynamoDB table so you can restore it to a specific point in time.
                        35-day recovery
                            can restore to a point within the previous 35 day
                    On-demand backup:
                        can keep that backup for a long time unlike PITR's 35 day.
                    Note:
                        AWS Backup:
                            can centrally manage backups for supported AWS services, including DynamoDB.
                            Cross-Region backup copies
                            can be useful for disaster recovery.
                recovery process creates a new table.
                |                    | PITR                                    | On-demand                               |
                | ------------------ | --------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
                | Backup style       | Continuous                              | Manual/explicit                         |
                | Recovery           | **Any point in recovery window**        | Restore from backup                     |
                | Retention          | **Up to 35 days**                       | Until explicitly deleted                |
                | Good for           | Accidental changes/deletes              | Long-term backup                        |
                | Performance impact | No significant table performance impact | No significant table performance impact |
                | Restore            | **Creates new table**                   | **Creates new table**                   |
            Integration with s3:
                DynamoDB → S3 = Export:
                    PITR required.
                        an export a consistent snapshot of the table at a selected point in time within the PITR window.
                    within the available 35-day recovery window.
                    NOT consume Read Capacity
                    Retain snapshots for auditing
                    ETL: Extract → Transform → Load
                    export format: DynamoDB JSON or Ion
                S3 → DynamoDB = Import
                    Import CSV, DynamoDB JSON or ION format
                    doesn't consume Write Capacity
                    Import creates a NEW table
                    Import errors are logged in CloudWatch Logs
                Summary:
                    |                    | Export to S3             | Import from S3                    |
                    | ------------------ | ------------------------ | --------------------------------- |
                    | Direction          | DynamoDB → S3            | S3 → DynamoDB                     |
                    | PITR required?     | **Yes**                  | No                                |
                    | Capacity consumed  | **No RCU**               | **No WCU**                        |
                    | Creates new table? | N/A                      | **Yes**                           |
                    | Formats            | DynamoDB JSON, Ion       | CSV, DynamoDB JSON, Ion           |
                    | Use case           | Analytics, auditing, ETL | Load/transform data into DynamoDB |
                    | Errors             | —                        | CloudWatch Logs                   |

                    Export → doesn't consume RCU
                    Import → doesn't consume WCU
    API Gateway:
        API Gateway = a managed "front door" for your APIs.
            Commonly used with AWS Lambda for serverless APIs
            No infrastructure to manage
            Supports HTTP/REST APIs and WebSocket APIs
            API versioning (v1, v2, ...)
            Different environments (dev, test, prod)
            Authentication & authorization integrations
            API keys + usage plans + throttling
            Import APIs using Swagger/OpenAPI
            Request/response transformation & validation
            Can generate client SDKs/API specifications
            API response caching
        API Gateway + Lambda:
            no infrastructure to manage.
            API Gateway handles the API-facing responsibilities, while Lambda handles the business logic.
        3 important integration types:
            Lambda:
                easy way to expose REST API backed by Lambda.
            HTTP:
                HTTP endpoint.
                Why put API Gateway in front of an existing HTTP API?
                    Because API Gateway gives you API-management features.
                    eg: Rate Limiting, Authentication, API Keys, Caching.
            AWS Service Integration:
                API Gateway doesn't have to call Lambda or an HTTP server.
                It can directly integrate with AWS services.
                eg: Send message to SQS, Start Step Functions.
            why is API Gateway being added?
                usually:
                    Authentication
                    Rate limiting / throttling
                    API keys
                    Caching
                    Public API
                    API management
        3 endpoint types:
            Global → Edge-Optimized:
                default.
                Global clients/ For clients around the world.
                The request first reaches a nearby CloudFront edge location, 
                    which can improve network latency.
                    Edge-Optimized does NOT mean API Gateway is deployed globally.
                API Gateway itself remains in one AWS Region
            Regional → Regional:
                API Gateway is accessed directly in its AWS Region.
                when Your clients are primarily located in the same Region.
                instead of Edge-Optimized, => more control over CloudFront.
            VPC only → Private:
                can only be accessed from your VPC.
                For internal API (i.e: should not be Internet-accessible)
                How to connect Privarte API Gateway?
                    using Interface VPC Endpoint(ENI)
            Note:
                | Type               | Main purpose             | Client location | CloudFront                    |
                | ------------------ | ------------------------ | --------------- | ----------------------------- |
                | **Edge-Optimized** | Global users             | Worldwide       | Automatically uses CloudFront |
                | **Regional**       | Users in/near one Region | Regional        | Can add your own CloudFront   |
                | **Private**        | Internal APIs            | **VPC only**    | Not public CloudFront access  |
        Security:
            User Authentication:
                IAM:
                    IAM Role: for AWS/internal applications.
                    eg:
                        Internal AWS application
                        AWS service calling API Gateway
                        IAM permissions
                        AWS credentials
                        SigV4 authentication
                Cognito:
                    external application users
                    Amazon Cognito is designed for managing identities for application users.
                    for mobile/WEB application:
                        Mobile App
                        ↓
                        Cognito
                        ↓
                        JWT token
                        ↓
                        API Gateway
                        ↓
                        Lambda
                Custom Authorizer:
                    Sometimes IAM or Cognito doesn't fit your requirements.
                    for own authentication/authorization logic.
                    could have your own token system.
            Custom Domain + HTTPS:
                HTTPS Security with ACM:
                    If using Edge-Optimized endpoint, then the certificate must be in us-east-l
                    If using Regional endpoint, the certificate must be same Region as API Gateway.
                    DNS: 
                        Use Route 53
                        Must create a CNAME or Alias record pointing your custom domain to API Gateway.
        Note:
            API Gateway receives the client's API request, then forwards/integrates that request to a backend.
    AWS Step Functions:
        Step Functions = coordinator
            think: "Run A first. If A succeeds, run B. Then run C."
            while:  Lambda = worker
        a managed workflow orchestrator that coordinates multiple steps/services in a process.
        controls which step runs, when it runs, and what happens if something fails.
            Step Functions
                │
                ├── Lambda: Check Payment
                │
                ├── Lambda: Check Inventory
                │
                ├── Lambda: Reserve Item
                │
                ├── Lambda: Shipping
                │
                └── Lambda: Send Email
        Orchestrate = oordinate multiple tasks and control their execution order.
        features:
            Sequence: can execute tasks sequentially.
            Parallel: don't depend on each other.can happen in parallel.
            Conditions / Choice: can make decisions in the workflow.
            Error handling:  can define what should happen
            Timeouts: can also control how long a task is allowed to run.
            Human approval: A human can make a decision as part of the workflow.
        Can integrate with EC2, ECS, On-premises servers, API Gateway, SQS queues, etc.
            not only Lambda.
    Amazon Cognito:
        a managed identity service for your application users.
        Serverless user database:
            Create a serverless database of users for your web & mobile apps
            Suppose you have a mobile application with 10,000 users:
            Cognito User Pool
            │
            ├── User A
            ├── User B
            ├── User C
            ├── ...
            └── User 10,000
            Cognito manages the user identities for you.
        TWO major concepts:
            User Pools:
                think: "Who is my user?" / Sign-in
                User directory
                Integrate with API Gateway
                    very common architecture:
                            Mobile/Web App
                                ↓
                            Cognito User Pool
                                ↓
                            Authentication
                                ↓
                            API Gateway
                                ↓
                            Lambda
                Integrate with Application Load Balancers:
                    User
                     ↓
                    ALB
                    ↓
                    Cognito authentication
                    ↓
                    EC2
            Identity Pools:
                think: "What AWS credentials can this user get?"
                can provide temporary AWS credentials.
                The user can then access an AWS resource according to 
                    the permissions associated with those credentials.
            Note:
                Identity Pools can use User Pools.
                Cognito is designed for this kind of large-scale application identity management.
                    Key: "Hundreds/thousands/millions of application users"
                SAML authentication:
                    Users can authenticate using an existing identity system
                        rather than creating another username/password account.
                        Cognito can be part of that federation architecture.

                |                               | User Pool          | Identity Pool                       |
                | ----------------------------- | ------------------ | ----------------------------------- |
                | Main purpose                  | **Authentication** | **AWS access**                      |
                | Manages users                 | ✅                  | Not primarily                       |
                | Sign-in/sign-up               | ✅                  | ❌                                   |
                | Provides AWS credentials      | ❌                  | ✅                                   |
                | Access AWS resources directly | ❌                  | ✅                                   |
                | Works with API Gateway        | ✅                  | Can be part of broader architecture |
                | Temporary AWS credentials     | ❌                  | ✅                                   |
Serverless Solution Architecture Discussion:
    Mobile application: MyTodoList
        requirements:
            Expose as REST API with HTTPS
            Serverless architecture
            Users should be able to directly interact with their own folder in S3
            Users should authenticate through a managed serverless service
            The users can write and read to-dos, but they mostly read them
        Discussion:
            REST API + HTTPS => API Gateway.
                API Gateway gives us:
                    REST API
                    HTTPS endpoint
                    Authentication/authorization integration
                    Throttling
                    API management
            Serverless architecture:
                API Gateway -> Lambda -> DynamoDB
                We don't want EC2.
            User authentication:
                Cognito User Pool.
            Store To-Dos:
                DynamoDB
            mostly read/high read throughput:
                DAX = in-memory cache for DynamoDB
            Direct access to user's S3 folder:
                Cognito Identity Pool.
            Note:
                Why not use Lambda to access S3?
                    requirement:
                        Users should be able to directly interact with their own folder in S3
                    So, The mobile application accesses S3 directly using temporary credentials from Cognito Identity Pools.
                How do we make sure users only access their own folder?
                    Imagine the S3 structure:
                        S3 Bucket
                        │
                        ├── user-001/
                        │   ├── photo1.jpg
                        │   └── photo2.jpg
                        │
                        ├── user-002/
                        │   ├── photo3.jpg
                        │   └── photo4.jpg
                        │
                        └── user-003/

                    We want:
                        User 001 → user-001/ only
                        User 002 → user-002/ only
                        User 003 → user-003/ only

                        Cognito Identity Pools can work with IAM roles to provide appropriately scoped temporary permissions.

                        Conceptually:
                            User
                            ↓
                            Cognito
                            ↓
                            Temporary AWS credentials
                            ↓
                            IAM role
                            ↓
                            S3
                            ↓
                            Only user's folder
            Complete architecture:
                                    📱 MyTodoList
                                        │
                            ┌────────────┴────────────┐
                            │                         │
                            ↓                         ↓
                    Cognito User Pool             API Gateway
                    Authentication                 HTTPS
                            │                         │
                            ↓                         ↓
                    Cognito Identity Pool           Lambda
                            │                         │
                    Temporary AWS credentials         ↓
                            │                        DAX
                            ↓                         ↓
                            S3                    DynamoDB
    Serverless hosted website: MyBlog.com:
        requirements:
            This website should scale globally
            Blogs are rarely written, but often read
            Some of the website is purely static files, the rest is a dynamic REST API
            Caching must be implement where possible
            Any new users that subscribes should receive a welcome email
            Any photo uploaded to the blog should have a thumbnail generated
        Discussion:
            Website should scale globally:
                Amazon CloudFront.
                CloudFront is a global CDN.
                for domain, Route 53 + CloudFront.
            Mostly read, rarely written:
                we want caching.
                Static content → S3 + CloudFront
                on S3, Pucket Policy must only authorize from CloudFront Distribution.
            Dynamic REST API:
                API Gateway + Lambda.
                The REST API was serverless, didn't need Cognito because public.
            Caching must be implemented:
                static files: CloudFront -> s3 (CloudFront caches static content globally.)
                API responses: API responses can provide caching for API responses. OR
                DAX.
                    Good for: Blogs are rarely written but frequently read.
            New users should receive a welcome email:
                should be event-driven: Amazon SES (Simple Email Service)
                sample:
                    User Registration
                        ↓
                        Cognito
                        ↓
                    Post Confirmation Trigger
                        ↓
                        Lambda
                        ↓
                        SES
                        ↓
                    Welcome Email
            Photo upload → thumbnail:
                S3 + Lambda
            User Welcome email flow:
                when data store on DynamoDB, DynamoDB Stream -> Lambda -> SES.
            Note:
                when you see Caching,
                    Possible AWS services:
                        CloudFront
                        API Gateway caching
                        ElastiCache, depending on architecture
                But don't confuse DAX with CloudFront:
                There can be multiple caching layers:
                    User
                    │
                    ▼
                    CloudFront
                    │
                    │ Cache hit
                    ├──────────────► Static content
                    │
                    ▼
                    API Gateway
                    │
                    ▼
                    Lambda
                    │
                    ▼
                    DAX
                    │
                    │ Cache hit
                    ├──────────────► Blog data
                    │
                    ▼
                    DynamoDB
                Differences purposes on cache:
                    | Cache                 | Caches                        | Purpose                       |
                    | --------------------- | ----------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
                    | **CloudFront**        | Static files / HTTP responses | Global CDN                    |
                    | **API Gateway Cache** | API responses                 | Reduce API backend calls      |
                    | **DAX**               | DynamoDB data                 | Extremely fast DynamoDB reads |
                Why each service?
                    | Requirement               | Service                              |
                    | ------------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
                    | Global website            | **CloudFront**                       |
                    | Static files              | **S3**                               |
                    | Secure S3 origin          | **CloudFront OAC**                   |
                    | Public REST API           | **API Gateway**                      |
                    | Serverless API logic      | **Lambda**                           |
                    | Frequently read blog data | **DAX**                              |
                    | Global database           | **DynamoDB Global Tables**           |
                    | Cache static/API content  | **CloudFront / API Gateway caching** |
                    | Welcome email             | **SES + Lambda**                     |
                    | Thumbnail generation      | **S3 Event → Lambda → S3**           |
        Complete Architecture:
                        Route 53
                           │
                           ▼
                      CloudFront
                       /       \
                      /         \
                Static           API
                  │               │
                  ▼               ▼
                 S3          API Gateway
                                  │
                                  ▼
                               Lambda
                                  │
                                  ▼
                                 DAX
                                  │
                                  ▼
                       DynamoDB Global Tables
                         /       |        \
                        /        |         \
                    Tokyo    Singapore      US
                                │
                                ▼
                            DynamoDB Steam
                                │
                                ▼
                            Lambda
                                │
                                ▼
                            SES
                                │
                                ▼
                            Welcome Email


                            Photo Upload
                                │
                                ▼
                                S3
                                │
                                │ Event
                                ▼
                                Lambda
                                │
                                ▼
                            Thumbnail
                                │
                                ▼
                                S3
    Micro Services Architecture:
        Instead of building one huge application:
            Monolithic Application
            ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
            │ User │ Order │ Payment │ Shipping │ ... │
            └─────────────────────────────────────────┘
            we split it into small, independent services.
            Each microservice is responsible for one business capability.
            example:
                User Service → users
                Order Service → orders
                Payment Service → payments
                Shipping Service → shipping
        You are free to design each microservice the way you want.
            Each service can use different: languaes, dbs, OS, etc..
            called technological freedom / service autonomy.
        Many services interact with each other directly using a REST API
        polyglot architecture:
            Each architecture for each micro service may vary.
        How do microservices communicate?
            Synchronous:
                Synchronous = request → wait → response
                "I send a request and wait for your response."
            Asynchronous:
                Async → Message/Event
                "I send the message, and I don't have to wait for the other service to finish."
        Challenge:
            Repeated overhead:
                Imagine your company has 50 microservices.

                For every service you may need:

                Create infrastructure
                Configure networking
                Configure security
                Configure deployment
                Configure monitoring
                Configure scaling
                Configure servers

                Doing this 50 times creates a lot of operational work.

                Service 1 → infrastructure
                Service 2 → infrastructure
                Service 3 → infrastructure
                ...
                Service 50 → infrastructure

                This is a major challenge.
            Server density / utilization:
                Suppose each microservice needs its own EC2 capacity.
                    EC2
                    ┌──────────────────────────┐
                    │ Service A       10% CPU  │
                    │ Service B       15% CPU  │
                    │ Service C        5% CPU  │
                    └──────────────────────────┘

                    You may have servers that aren't being efficiently utilized.

                    Some services are busy:

                    Service A → 90% CPU

                    while others are almost idle:

                    Service B → 5% CPU

                    You have to manage how workloads are placed and scaled.
            Multiple versions:
                Suppose you have:

                Order Service

                You release:
                    v1
                    v2
                    v3

                But users aren't all migrated at once.

                You may need:
                    Client A → Order Service v1
                    Client B → Order Service v2
                    Client C → Order Service v3

                And imagine doing that for 50 microservices.

                That's a lot of version management.
                    Service A → v1, v2
                    Service B → v1, v2, v3
                    Service C → v2, v3
                    Service D → v1, v3
                ...
                This becomes operationally complex.
            Client-side complexity:
                Imagine a mobile application needs to communicate with:
                    User Service
                    Order Service
                    Payment Service
                    Shipping Service
                    Notification Service
                    Product Service

                    The client now needs to know about many APIs:

                    Mobile App
                    ├── User API
                    ├── Order API
                    ├── Payment API
                    ├── Shipping API
                    ├── Notification API
                    └── Product API

                    That's a lot of client-side integration.

                    The client has to understand:
                        Different endpoints
                        Authentication
                        API versions
                        Request formats
                        Response formats
                        Error handling
        How Serverless helps:
            AWS serverless services can reduce many of these operational problems.
        Swagger / OpenAPI integration:
            Generated client SDK through Swagger integration for the API Gateway.
            This solves the client-side complexity problem.
        Software updates offloading/Distribute:
            Problem:
                Imagine your EC2 application distributes a software update:
                                EC2
                                │
                        ┌─────────┼─────────┐
                        ↓         ↓         ↓
                    User 1    User 2    User 3
                        ↓         ↓         ↓
                        Software Update

                    Normally, the EC2 instance has to send the same large file again and again.

                    If 10,000 users download a 500 MB update:
                        EC2 → 500 MB
                        EC2 → 500 MB
                        EC2 → 500 MB
                        ...
                        EC2 → 500 MB × 10,000 users

                    This causes:
                        High CPU usage
                        High network bandwidth
                        High EC2 costs
                        Potential performance problems during update releases
                Solution:
                    Don't change the application → Put CloudFront in front.
                    Use Amazon CloudFront to cache and distribute the software updates.
                    Why does this reduce EC2 CPU?
                        Without CloudFront:
                            1000 users
                                ↓
                            EC2
                                ↓
                            1000 downloads

                        With CloudFront:
                            1000 users
                                ↓
                            CloudFront
                                ↓
                            Maybe only a few requests to EC2
                        CloudFront handles the distribution.
                        The content is distributed from CloudFront's global network.
                        So you are offloading traffic from the origin.
                        The keyword is:⭐ Offloading
                    don't need to rewrite the EC2 application.
                        Before: Users → EC2
                        After: Users → CloudFront → EC2
                    Why not S3?
                        if you're allowed to change the application/architecture, could store on s3.
Databases:
    a lot of databases.
    To choose right database:
        AWS Databases
            │
            ├── Relational
            │   ├── RDS
            │   └── Aurora
            │
            ├── NoSQL
            │   ├── DynamoDB
            │   └── DocumentDB
            │
            ├── Cache
            │   └── ElastiCache
            │
            ├── Data Warehouse
            │   └── Redshift
            │
            ├── Graph
            │   └── Neptune
            │
            ├── Time Series
            │   └── Timestream
            │
            └── Search
                └── OpenSearch
        What questions should I ask before choosing a database?
            Question 1: Read-heavy or write-heavy?
                What kind of workload do we have?
                Read-heavy:
                    Example:
                        Product catalog
                        News website
                        Social media feed
                Write-heavy:
                    Example:
                        IoT sensors
                        Logging
                        Transaction processing
                Balanced:
                    Many normal applications are like this.
            Question 2: Throughput requirements:
                How much data needs to be processed per second? request rates?
                example:
                    Application A
                    → 100 requests/sec

                    Application B
                    → 100,000 requests/sec

                    Application C
                    → millions of requests/sec
            Question 3: Does traffic fluctuate?
                Imagine:
                    09:00 → 1,000 requests
                    12:00 → 5,000 requests
                    18:00 → 100,000 requests
                    23:00 → 1,000 requests

                    This is a fluctuating workload.
                    You should think about databases/services that can scale automatically.
            Question 4: — How much data?
                How much data do we need to store?
                example:
                    Small:
                    10 GB

                    Medium:
                    1 TB

                    Large:
                    100 TB

                    Massive:
                    Petabytes

                Also ask: Will the data grow?

                example:
                    Today → 1 TB
                    Next year → 20 TB
                    Next 5 years → 500 TB
            Question 5: How long do we need to keep the data?
                How much data to store and for how long?
                example:
                    Logs
                    Maybe:
                        Hot logs → 7 days
                        Archive → 1 year
            Question 6: Average object size:
                Suppose you have:
                    1 million objects

                    But each object is: 5 KB

                    versus:
                        1 million objects
                        ×
                        500 MB each

                    These are completely different storage problems.
                    remember: Maximum DynamoDB item size = 400 KB.
                    So: S3 → large objects, DynamoDB → metadata
            Question 7: — How is the data accessed?
                How will the application access the data?
                eg: 
                    Key-value access  => DynamoDB
                    SQL queries   => Relational Database
                    Search  => OpenSearch
                    Graph relationships   > Neptune.
                Joins?
                    if use join, That's a relational database use case.
            Question 8: Structured(Strong schema) vs Semi-Structured data(flexibility)
                Structured:
                    Data follows a well-defined schema.
                Semi-structured:
                    Thet structure is more flexible.
                    NoSQL/document databases.
            Question 9: Reporting
                Suppose you have millions/billions of records and want analytical queries:
                    Total sales by country
                    Total sales by month
                    Average revenue
                    Revenue by product

                That's not necessarily your transactional database's job.

                Think: Amazon Redshift
            Question 10: Search?
                "Users need fast full-text search."  => Amazon OpenSearch Service.
            Question 11: Latency requirements?
                How quickly must the database respond?
                Normal: 100 ms.
                    Maybe a normal database is fine.
                Very low latency:
                    single-digit milliseconds
                    DynamoDB is designed for this kind of predictable low-latency access.
                Microseconds:
                    DAX / ElastiCache
            Question 12: Concurrent users
                How many users will access the database at the same time?
                example:
                    10 users
                    100 users
                    10,000 users
                A database architecture designed for 10 users may not be appropriate for millions of concurrent requests.
            Question 13: Data durability/Source Of Truth:
                Data durability? Source of truth for the data?
            Question 14: License costs
                Can we move to a cloud-native AWS database instead?"
                This usually comes up when migrating an existing database.
                Suppose your on-premises application uses a commercial database with expensive licensing.
            Switch to Cloud Native DB such as Aurora?
                example:
                    Traditional database
                            ↓
                    Migration
                            ↓
                    Amazon Aurora

                    Aurora is AWS's cloud-optimized relational database compatible with:

                    MySQL
                    PostgreSQL
                simple decision process:
                                What kind of data?
                                    ↓
                            ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                            ↓                     ↓
                    Relational?              NoSQL?
                            ↓                     ↓
                    RDS/Aurora             DynamoDB
            Exam mindset:
                ① What is the data model?
                    Relational?
                    Key-value?
                    Document?
                    Graph?
                    Time series?
                ② How will I query it?
                    JOINs?
                    Key lookup?
                    Full-text search?
                    Analytics?
                ③ What's the workload?
                    Read-heavy?
                    Write-heavy?
                    Balanced?
                    High throughput?
                    Fluctuating?
                ④ What's the latency?
                    Milliseconds?
                    Single-digit milliseconds?
                    Microseconds?
                ⑤ How much data?
                    GB?
                    TB?
                    PB?
                    Large individual objects?
                ⑥ How important is scalability?
                    10 users?
                    10,000?
                    Millions?
                ⑦ What are the operational/cost requirements?
                    Managed?
                    Serverless?
                    Licensing?
                    Maintenance?
            DatabaseType:
                RDBMS (= SQL / OLTP): RDS, Aurora - great for joins
                NoSQL database - no joins, no SQL : DynamoDB (~JSON), ElastiCache (key / value pairs), Neptune (graphs), DocumentDB (for MongoDB), Keyspaces (for Apache Cassandra)
                Object Store: S3 (for big objects) / Glacier (for backups / archives)
                Data Warehouse (= SQL Analytics / BI): Redshift (OLAP), Athena, EMR
                Search: OpenSearch (SON) - free text, unstructured searches
                Graphs: Amazon Neptune - displays relationships between data
                Ledger: Amazon Quantum Ledger Database
                Time series: Amazon Timestream
    RDS:
        AWS-managed relational database.
            ❌ EC2
            ↓
            Install MySQL
            Configure MySQL
            Patch OS
            ...
        DS does much of that management for you:
            ✅ Amazon RDS
                ↓
            AWS manages infrastructure
                ↓
            You manage the database configuration/data
        You still choose the instance size
            doesn't mean "no servers exist."
        Storage Auto Scaling
            automatically increase its storage when your database is running out of space.
        Read Replicas
            Scaling READ traffic
        Multi-AZ:
            Main purpose: High Availability / failover
            Primary/Standby flow.
            |              | Read Replica                  | Multi-AZ                                 |
            | ------------ | ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
            | Main purpose | **Read scaling**              | **High availability**                    |
            | Reads?       | Yes, replicas can serve reads | Standby normally isn't for serving reads |
            | Replication  | Asynchronous                  | Synchronous                              |
            | Failover     | Not automatic in the same way | **Automatic failover**                   |
            | Primary goal | Performance                   | Availability                             |
        RDS Security: Security Groups, IAM, KMS, SSL/TLS
        Automated Backups + Point-in-Time Recovery
            up to 35 days
            The restore creates a new RDS database instance
        Manual DB Snapshot
        Secrets Manager integration:
            instead of directly push on app : DB_PASSWORD="mypassword123"
            can use:
                Application
                    ↓
                Secrets Manager
                    ↓
                DB credentials
                    ↓
                RDS
        RDS Custom:
            sometimes: we need more control on DB: We need access to the underlying OS/database environment and need to customize it.
            for Oracle or SQL Server.
    Aurora:
        MySQL/PostgreSQL compatibility + better cloud-native architecture

                Aurora Cluster
                /           \
            Compute         Storage
                ↓                ↓
            DB Instances    Shared storage

            The storage layer is managed separately from the database compute instances.
            This allows Aurora to scale and manage storage differently from compute.
        6 copies across 3 AZs
            Self-healing storage
            If a storage copy has a problem:
                Copy ① ❌
                ↓
                Aurora automatically repairs/rebuilds it
        Auto-scaling storage:
        Aurora Compute
        Aurora Read Replicas
        Custom Endpoints
            Aurora provides endpoints so your application doesn't need to know the individual instance addresses.
            Two important concepts:
                Writer endpoint
                    sends traffic to the current writer.
                Reader endpoint
                    distributes read traffic among available reader instances.
        RDS features are still available.
        Aurora Serverless
            Unpredictable/intermittent workloads
        Aurora Global Database
            Multiple AWS Regions
        Aurora Machine Learning
            Integrates with SageMaker / Comprehend
        Aurora Database Cloning
            Quickly create a new cluster from an existing Aurora cluster
        Security/Monitoring/Maintenance:
            Similar capabilities to RDS
        Note:
            AURORA
                │
                ├── 6 copies / 3 AZs
                ├── Shared distributed storage
                ├── Serverless → unpredictable workload
                ├── Global → multi-region
                └── Clone → fast copy
    ElastiCache:
        managed in-memory cache
        Supports:
            Redis
            Memcached
        Performance
            Data stored in RAM
            Sub-millisecond latency
        Common use cases
            Cache frequently accessed database queries
            Key-value data
            Session storage
            Reduce database read load
            Improve application latency
        Important
            Not a relational SQL database
            Usually not the source of truth
            Requires application changes to implement caching logic
        Redis
            Clustering
            Replication
            Read replicas
            High availability
        Security
            Security Groups
            KMS
            Authentication
            Encryption
        Backup
            Snapshots / backups available depending on engine/configuration
        Exam shortcuts
            "Frequent reads + same data repeatedly requested"
            → ElastiCache
            "Store website sessions across multiple servers"
            → ElastiCache
            "Sub-millisecond latency"
            → ElastiCache
            "DynamoDB + caching"
            → DAX
            "SQL + JOINs + source of truth"
            → RDS/Aurora
            "Cannot use SQL"
            → ElastiCache / DynamoDB / other NoSQL depending on context
    DynamoDB:
        | Question says...                   | Think...           |
        | ---------------------------------- | ------------------ |
        | **Serverless NoSQL**               | DynamoDB           |
        | **Massive scale**                  | DynamoDB           |
        | **Key-value / document**           | DynamoDB           |
        | **Single-digit milliseconds**      | DynamoDB           |
        | **Unpredictable traffic**          | DynamoDB On-Demand |
        | **Need cache for DynamoDB**        | DAX                |
        | **Microsecond reads**              | DAX                |
        | **Item expiration**                | DynamoDB TTL       |
        | **React to item changes**          | DynamoDB Streams   |
        | **Lambda triggered by DB changes** | DynamoDB Streams   |
        | **Multiple regions, read + write** | Global Tables      |
        | **Active-Active**                  | Global Tables      |
        | **Recover to a specific time**     | PITR               |
        | **Export database data to S3**     | DynamoDB Export    |
        | **No RCU during export**           | DynamoDB → S3      |
        | **No WCU during import**           | S3 → DynamoDB      |
        | **Schema changes frequently**      | DynamoDB           |
        | **SQL / JOINs**                    | RDS / Aurora       |
        | **Sub-millisecond cache**          | ElastiCache        |
    s3:
        Key/Value Store for Objects
            Key → Object
                photos/yan/profile.jpg → [image data]
                videos/intro.mp4       → [video data]
                documents/passport.pdf → [PDF data]
        Great for bigger objects, not so great for many small objects
        Max object size = 50 TB
        S3 is Serverless
            | Keyword in question                          | Think                 |
            | -------------------------------------------- | --------------------- |
            | **Object storage**                           | S3                    |
            | **Bucket**                                   | S3                    |
            | **50 TB object**                             | S3                    |
            | **Serverless storage**                       | S3                    |
            | **Images / videos / PDFs / backups**         | S3                    |
            | **Frequently accessed**                      | S3 Standard           |
            | **Infrequent access**                        | S3 Standard-IA        |
            | **Unknown access pattern**                   | Intelligent-Tiering   |
            | **Archive**                                  | Glacier               |
            | **Automatically move objects between tiers** | Lifecycle             |
            | **Protect old versions**                     | Versioning            |
            | **KMS encryption**                           | SSE-KMS               |
            | **Browser accessing S3 from another origin** | CORS                  |
            | **Prevent modification/deletion**            | Object Lock           |
            | **Large file upload**                        | Multipart Upload      |
            | **Users far from bucket**                    | Transfer Acceleration |
            | **Filter part of object**                    | S3 Select             |
            | **Operate on millions of objects**           | S3 Batch Operations   |
            | **Get inventory/listing**                    | S3 Inventory          |
            | **S3 object changed → Lambda**               | S3 Event Notification |
            | **Shared filesystem for ECS**                | EFS                   |
            | **EC2 disk**                                 | EBS                   |
            | **Database records**                         | DynamoDB/RDS          |
    DocumentDB:
        AWS cloud-optimized document database
        MongoDB compatible/migration.
        MongoDB/DocumentDB works with documents.
            JSON-like documents
        DocumentDB stores JSON-like data.
        Cluster architecture
            Similar deployment concepts as Aurora
                think of:
                        DocumentDB Cluster
                                │
                    ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                    ↓                     ↓
                Primary              Replicas
                    │                     │
                WRITE                  READ
                    │                     │
                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                                ↓
                        Shared Storage
        Highly Available across 3 AZs
        Storage automatically grows
        fully managed.
        DocumentDB Vs Aurora:
            |                 | Aurora                  | DocumentDB                 |
            | --------------- | ----------------------- | -------------------------- |
            | Database type   | Relational              | NoSQL document             |
            | Compatible with | **MySQL / PostgreSQL**  | **MongoDB**                |
            | Data model      | Tables/rows             | Documents                  |
            | Query style     | SQL                     | MongoDB-compatible APIs    |
            | Use case        | Relational applications | JSON/document applications |
            | HA              | Multi-AZ                | Multi-AZ                   |
            | Storage         | Distributed/shared      | Distributed/shared         |
            | Storage scaling | Automatic               | Automatic                  |
        DocumentDB vs DynamoDB:
            |                | DocumentDB                                | DynamoDB                |
            | -------------- | ----------------------------------------- | ----------------------- |
            | Type           | Document database                         | Key-value/document      |
            | AWS native     | Managed AWS service                       | **AWS proprietary**     |
            | Compatibility  | **MongoDB**                               | AWS API                 |
            | Serverless     | Not the same serverless model as DynamoDB | **Yes**                 |
            | Data           | JSON-like documents                       | Items/attributes        |
            | Primary access | MongoDB-compatible queries                | Primary key             |
            | Massive scale  | ✅                                         | **Excellent**           |
            | Use case       | MongoDB workloads                         | Serverless applications |
    Neptune:
        fully managed graph database.
        To efficiently represent and query connections between entities.
        Nodes + Relationships:
            User A ──friend──> User B
            │                  │
            likes              likes
            ↓                  ↓
            Post A ──comment──> Post B
            Nodes:
                Things/entities:
                    Users
                    Products
                    Posts
                    Accounts
                    Locations
            Relationships / Edges
                Connections between them:
                    friend
                    likes
                    follows
                    purchased
                    commented
                    transferred money to
            Why do we need Neptune?
                You want to answer: "Who are the friends of my friends who liked the same posts that I liked?"
                That's a relationship-heavy query.
                You may need to traverse:
                    Me
                    ↓
                    My friends
                    ↓
                    Their friends
                    ↓
                    Posts they liked
                    ↓
                    Common posts
                This can become very complicated in traditional relational databases.
            Exam Keyword:
                Highly connected data
                Graph database
                Relationships
                Social network:
                            User A
                            /      \
                        friend    likes
                        ↓          ↓
                        User B       Post 1
                        │
                        follows
                        ↓
                        User C
                        │
                        likes
                        ↓
                        Post 2
                Recommendation engine:
                    Graph:
                        User A
                        ├── bought → Product 1
                        ├── bought → Product 2
                        └── likes → Product 3

                        User B
                        ├── bought → Product 1
                        └── bought → Product 4

                        The system can traverse relationships:

                        You
                        ↓
                        similar users
                        ↓
                        their products
                        ↓
                        recommend
                Fraud detection:
                    Account A
                    ↓
                    transfers money to
                    ↓
                    Account B
                    ↓
                    transfers to
                    ↓
                    Account C
                    ↓
                    same device
                    ↓
                    Account D
                Knowledge graph:
                        Albert Einstein
                                │
                        born in
                                ↓
                            Germany
                                │
                        continent
                                ↓
                            Europe

                            Person
                            ↓
                            Company
                            ↓
                            Industry
                            ↓
                            Country
                            ↓
                            City
                Network topology
                Connected datasets(Huge number of connections/relationships)
            Neptune Vs DynamoDB:
                |                              | DynamoDB                          | Neptune                   |
                | ---------------------------- | --------------------------------- | ------------------------- |
                | Type                         | Key-value / document              | **Graph**                 |
