                                EventBridge
                                ↓
                                Lambda ❌ Bug
                            Events were archived.
                            After fixing Lambda:
                                Archive
                                │
                                │ Replay
                                ▼
                                EventBridge
                                ↓
                                Lambda ✅ Fixed
                            Now the old events can be processed again.
                Important:
                    on this EventBridge:
                        Don't think: Archive = backup of my application data.
                        Replay doesn't mean: "restore the application."
            Schema Registry:
                a place to store and manage the schemas of events.
                    schema:
                        the structure of an event — what fields exist and what type of data they contain.
                EventBridge can discover/infer schemas automatically
                    infer = ကောက်ချက်ချ
                Why is this useful?
                The biggest benefit is developer productivity.
                Generate code bindings from schemas
                schemas can be versioned.
        CloudWatch Container Insights:
            CloudWatch monitoring specifically designed for containers.
            supported:
                ECS
                    Amazon Elastic Container Service
                        ECS
                        ├── Task
                        │    └── Container
                    Container Insights can monitor ECS.
                EKS
                    Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service
                        EKS
                        ├── Node
                        │    └── Pod
                        │         └── Container
                    Container Insights can monitor EKS.
                Kubernetes on EC2
                    You can also run Kubernetes yourself on EC2 rather than using EKS.
                        EC2
                        └── Kubernetes
                            └── Containers
                    Container Insights can monitor these as well.
                Fargate
                    This is important because Fargate means you don't manage the underlying servers.
                    Container Insights supports:
                        ECS on Fargate
                        EKS on Fargate
                    So:
                                    Container Insights
                                            │
                            ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
                            ↓                           ↓
                            ECS                           EKS
                        ┌───────┐                    ┌───────┐
                        │ EC2   │                    │ EC2   │
                        │Fargate│                    │Fargate│
                        └───────┘                    └───────┘
            Containerized CloudWatch Agent:
                In Amazon EKS and Kubernetes,
                CloudWatch Insights is using a containerized version of the CloudWatch Agent to discover containers.
                    Kubernetes / EKS
                    │
                    ├── Application Container
                    │
                    ├── Application Container
                    │
                    └── CloudWatch Agent Container
                            ↓
                        CloudWatch
                The agent can discover the containers and collect their monitoring data.
                    "Discover containers" means what?
                    You don't necessarily need to manually tell CloudWatch:
                        "Monitor Container A"
                        "Monitor Container B"
                    The agent can discover the running container environment and collect relevant metrics/logs.
                    Container discovery = automatically identify the containers/workloads that need monitoring.
        CloudWatch Lambda Insights:
            "Monitor and troubleshoot Lambda applications with detailed system-level metrics and diagnostics."
            collects system-level metrics, such as:
                CPU time
                Memory
                Disk
                Network
                And diagnostic information such as:
                    Cold starts
                    Lambda worker shutdowns
            Lambda Insights is provided as a Lambda Layer.
                Lambda Insights → Lambda Layer
        CloudWatch Contributor Insights:
            who or what is contributing to the activity you're measuring.
            Top-N contributors:
                N = a number you choose.
                can find the largest contributors to a particular metric/activity.
            Top talkers:
                the resource generating the most activity.
                    Example: VPC traffic
                        Server A → 90%
                        Server B →  5%
                        Server C →  3%
                    Server A is the top talker.
                    So: Contributor Insights → find top talkers / top contributors.
            leverages your CloudWatch Logs
                leverages = use = အသုံးချ
        CloudWatch Application Insights:
            "I have a complete application made of many AWS resources. Something is wrong. Help me identify the problem."
            Imagine your application suddenly becomes slow.
                You have:
                    EC2
                    RDS
                    EBS
                    ELB
                    ASG
                    Lambda
                    SQS
                    DynamoDB
                    S3
                    API Gateway
                    ...
                You don't want to manually inspect every service.
                Application Insights helps provide a more application-focused view.
            Powered by SageMaker:
                automated application monitoring + troubleshooting.
            Application Insights = application-level troubleshooting
            EventBridge + SSM OpsCenter:
                Findings and alerts are sent to Amazon EventBridge and SSM OpsCenter.
                flow:
                            Application
                                ↓
                            Application Insights
                                ↓
                            Finding / Alert
                                │
                                ├──→ EventBridge
                                │
                                └──→ SSM OpsCenter
                SSM OpsCenter = A central place to manage operational issues/incidents.
        Note:
            when you see "top-N", "top talkers", "heaviest users", "URLs generating the most...", immediately think:
            ⭐ Contributor Insights

            When you see "cold starts, Lambda worker, Lambda memory/system metrics":
            ⭐ Lambda Insights

            When you see "application health, automated dashboards, troubleshoot ongoing application issues":
            ⭐ Application Insights
    CloudTrail:
        "WHO did WHAT, WHEN, and FROM WHERE in my AWS account?"
        It records API activity in your AWS account.
            User / Admin / Application
                │
                ├── AWS Console
                ├── AWS CLI
                ├── SDK
                └── AWS Service
                        │
                        ▼
                    CloudTrail
                        │
                        ▼
                Record the API call
            What does CloudTrail record?
            Suppose someone deletes an S3 bucket.
            CloudTrail can record information such as:
                WHO?
                → IAM user / role

                WHAT?
                → DeleteBucket

                WHEN?
                → 2026-08-26 10:30

                WHERE FROM?
                → Source IP

                WHICH RESOURCE?
                → my-important-bucket

            So if your boss asks:
            "Who deleted our production resource?"
            👉 CloudTrail
        CloudTrail is enabled by default.
        Everything is permanently stored automatically."
        For longer-term/custom logging, you normally configure a Trail and deliver events to storage such as S3, 
            and optionally integrate with CloudWatch Logs.
        CloudTrail → S3:
            Because you may want to keep audit logs for a long time
        CloudTrail → CloudWatch Logs:
            useful when you want to create monitoring/alerting workflows around API activity.
        "Resource deleted? Check CloudTrail first!"
        Governance / Compliance / Audit
        CloudWatch vs CloudTrail:
            |                  | CloudWatch                   | CloudTrail                                |
            | ---------------- | ---------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
            | Main purpose     | **Monitoring**               | **Auditing**                              |
            | Focus            | Performance / logs / metrics | API activity                              |
            | Question         | "How is my system?"          | "Who did this?"                           |
            | CPU usage        | ✅                            | ❌                                         |
            | Application logs | ✅                            | ❌                                         |
            | API calls        | ❌                            | ✅                                         |
            | User activity    | Limited                      | ✅                                         |
            | Audit/compliance | Supporting                   | ⭐ Main purpose                            |
            | Store in S3      | ✅                            | ✅                                         |
            | Alerting         | Alarm                        | Can integrate with CloudWatch/EventBridge |
        Management Events:
            "Someone changed my AWS infrastructure."
            Read vs Write Management Events:
                Read Event: just look. Doesn't change anything.
                Write Event: Can change something.
        Data Events:
            DATA inside those resources
            Lambda Data Events:
                can also record Lambda function execution activity
            Note:
                |                    | Management Events | Data Events               |
                | ------------------ | ----------------- | ------------------------- |
                | Focus              | Manage resources  | Access/use data           |
                | Example            | CreateSubnet      | GetObject                 |
                | Example            | AttachRolePolicy  | PutObject                 |
                | Example            | CreateTrail       | DeleteObject              |
                | Default            | Logged by trails  | **Not logged by default** |
                | Volume             | Generally lower   | Often very high           |
                | S3 object activity | ❌                 | ✅                         |
                | Lambda Invoke      | ❌                 | ✅                         |
        CloudTrail Insights:
            to detect: Unusual API activity / unusual behavior
            How does Insights work?
            Step 1 — Establish a baseline
            Step 2 — Continuously analyze activity
        What can Insights detect?
            Unusual resource provisioning
            Service limit problems
            Burst of IAM actions (IAM လုပ်ဆောင်ချက်များ ပေါက်ကွဲထွက်လာခြင်း)
            Gaps in periodic maintenance (ပုံမှန်ပြုပြင်ထိန်းသိမ်းမှုတွင် ကွာဟချက်များ)
        Where do Insights appear?
            │
            ├──→ CloudTrail console
            │
            ├──→ S3
            │
            └──→ EventBridge
            Anomalies appear in the CloudTrail console.
                Anomalies = ပုံမှန်မဟုတ်မှုများ
        Retention — 90 Days:
            if you need longer-term retention:
                CloudTrail
                    ↓
                Trail
                    ↓
                S3
                    ↓
                Long-term storage
            Then you can use Athena to query those CloudTrail logs.
                CloudTrail
                    ↓
                S3
                    ↓
                Athena
                    ↓
                SQL query
            Example:
            "Show me all DeleteBucket events from the last 2 years."
            👉 CloudTrail logs stored in S3 + Athena
        Remember:
            Management = RESOURCE
            Data = DATA
            Insights = ANOMALY
    AWS Config:
        main idea: record resource configuration + track changes + evaluate compliance.
        think: What is the configuration/state of my resources, and does it comply with my rules?
        Compliance = Does this resource follow the rule?
        AWS Config is heavily associated with:
            Governance
            Compliance
            Security
            Auditing
            Configuration management
        as a configuration auditor for your AWS resources.
            Imagine your company has a rule:
            "All S3 buckets must not be publicly accessible."
            You have 500 S3 buckets.
            Manually checking:
                Bucket 1 → public?
                Bucket 2 → public?
                Bucket 3 → public?
                ...
                Bucket 500 → public?
                😵
            AWS Config can evaluate this automatically.
                S3 Buckets
                    ↓
                AWS Config Rule
                "Bucket must not be public"
                    ↓
                ┌─────────────────────┐
                │ Bucket A → COMPLIANT│
                │ Bucket B → COMPLIANT│
                │ Bucket C → NON-COMPLIANT
                └─────────────────────┘
            That's the core purpose.
        Configuration History:
            keeps a history of resource configuration changes.
        Config Resource:
            View compliance of a resource over time
            View configuration of a resource over time
            View CloudTrail API calls of a resource over time
        Config vs CloudTrail:
            CloudTrail asks: WHO made the change?
            AWS Config asks: WHAT was the configuration before and after the change?
        Config Rules:
            defines a condition that your resources should satisfy.
            Managed Rules vs Custom Rules
                AWS provides many managed Config Rules that you can use without writing the rule yourself.
                You can also create custom rules.
            When are Config Rules evaluated?
                two important triggers:
                    Resource configuration changes
                                ↓
                        Config Rule
                            evaluates
                OR
                    Periodic schedule
                        ↓
                    Config Rule
                    evaluates
        Config Rules DON'T prevent actions:
            Config does not automatically block the API request.
                Change happens
                    ↓
                AWS Config evaluates
                    ↓
                NON-COMPLIANT
                    ↓
                Remediation (if configured)
        Regional:
            a per-region service. So, to view centrally, can use Config Aggregator.
        Config Aggregator:
            to collect configuration/compliance information from multiple accounts and Regions.
        Remediation:
            AWS Config can automatically remediate a NON_COMPLIANT resource using SSM Automation Documents.
            remediation = ပြုပြင်မွမ်းမံခြင်း
                Config Rule
                │
                │ evaluates
                ↓
                Target Resource
                │
                └── NON_COMPLIANT
                        ↓
                    Remediation
                        ↓
                Change TARGET RESOURCE
                        ↓
                    COMPLIANT
                eg:
                    Suppose you have this Config Rule:
                    EBS volumes must be encrypted
                    The rule itself doesn't change.
                        Config Rule:
                        "EBS must be encrypted"
                                │
                                ↓
                        EBS Volume A
                        encrypted = false
                                │
                                ↓
                        NON_COMPLIANT
                                │
                                ↓
                        Remediation
                                │
                                ↓
                        Fix EBS Volume A
                                │
                                ↓
                        encrypted = true
                                │
                                ↓
                        COMPLIANT
            Remediation Retries:
                You can set Remediation Retries if the resource is still non-compliant
            Notification:
                Config → SNS
                Config → EventBridge
            Pricing:
                a paid service; configuration recording and rule evaluations can incur charges.
IAM Advanced:
    AWS Organizations:
        one place to manage multiple AWS accounts.
                         AWS Organization
                            │
                    Management Account
                            │
                ┌────────────┼────────────┐
                ▼            ▼            ▼
                OU A         OU B       OU C
                │            │            │
            Accounts      Accounts    Accounts
        OU = Organization Units (group of accounts)
            Whey OUs?
                group/container of AWS Accounts:
                    AWS Organization
                        │
                        ├── Security OU
                        │     ├── Security Account
                        │     └── Audit Account
                        │
                        ├── Production OU
                        │     ├── Production-1
                        │     └── Production-2
                        │
                        └── Development OU
                            ├── Dev-1
                            └── Dev-2
                    for eg: "All Production accounts must not use this AWS service."
        Management Account:
            main account of the Organization.
            manages the organization and has special organization-level permissions.
        Member Account:
            belongs to one organization at a time.
        Consolidated Billing:
            major benefit.
            instead of 
                Account A → Bill
                Account B → Bill
                Account C → Bill
            organization can provide:
                Account A ─┐
                Account B ─┤
                Account C ─┼──→ Consolidated billing
                Account D ─┘
                                ↓
                            One payer
            gives the organization a centralized billing arrangement.
        Aggregated usage:
            some AWS pricing benefits can be based on aggregated usage.
            organization may receive volume pricing benefits.
                Account A → 100 GB
                Account B → 200 GB
        Reserved Instances & Savings Plans:
            can also allow eligible discounts from things such as:
                Reserved Instances
                Savings Plans
            to benefit workloads across accounts, depending on the applicable sharing settings and service.
        Advantages:
            Tagging standards for billing:
                can use tags to identify resources.
                    Environment = Production
                    Department  = Finance
                    Project      = Website
            Centralized CloudTrail:
                Instead of keeping audit logs separately, you can create a dedicated logging/security account and centrally collect logs.
            Central CloudWatch Logs:
                can design centralized logging
            Cross-account IAM Roles
                Suppose you're an administrator and need to access the Production account.
                You don't necessarily create the same admin user in every account.
                using AssumeRole.
        SCP — Service Control Policy:
            organization-level policy
            used to define the maximum permissions available to accounts/users/roles under it.
            AWS Account တစ်ခုမှာ "အများဆုံး ဘာတွေလုပ်ခွင့်ရှိမလဲ" ဆိုတာကို ကန့်သတ်ထားတဲ့ Organization-level စည်းမျဉ်း
            SCP does NOT grant permissions:
            not like an IAM policy that grants access.
                IAM Policy
                    ↓
                Can grant permission

                SCP
                    ↓
                Can restrict permission
                🔴 SCP က Permission ပေးတာ မဟုတ်ဘူး။
                🟢 SCP က Permission ကို ကန့်သတ်တာ။
                SCP = organizational guardrail
                eg: Company policy: "Nobody in Development can use certain AWS services."
                    SCP ကို "Company Gate" လို့ မှတ်ပါ 🚪

                    ဒီလိုစဉ်းစားပါ။
                        Developer
                            ↓
                        ┌─────────────────┐
                        │   SCP Gate 🚪   │
                        │                 │
                        │ EC2 allowed?    │
                        └─────────────────┘
                            ↓
                        ┌─────────────────┐
                        │   IAM Policy    │
                        │                 │
                        │ User allowed?   │
                        └─────────────────┘
                            ↓
                            AWS Resource
                    နှစ်ခုလုံး OK ဖြစ်မှ လုပ်လို့ရတယ်။

                    SCP  → Allowed
                    IAM  → Allowed
                        ↓
                        ✅
            SCP inheritance through OUs:
                can attach an SCP at the Root or Production OU.
                An SCP attached higher in the hierarchy can restrict everything underneath it.
                SCP က Account တစ်ခုကို "အပေါ်ကနေ" ထိန်းတယ်
                    Development accounts အားလုံးမှာ rule တစ်ခုတည်း ထားချင်တယ်။
                    "Region တချို့မှာ resource မဖန်တီးရ"
                    SCP ကို Development OU မှာ attach လုပ်လိုက်ရုံပဲ။
                        Root SCP
                        ↓
                        OU SCP
                        ↓
                        Account
                        ↓
                        User / Role
                        ဆိုပြီး restriction က အောက်ကို သက်ရောက်နိုင်တယ်။
                        SCP က Firewall လိုမျိုး နည်းနည်းဆင်တယ်။
                            Firewall က: "ဒီ traffic ကို ဖြတ်ခွင့်ရှိလား?"
                            SCP က: "ဒီ AWS Account က ဒီ action ကို လုပ်ခွင့်ရှိနိုင်လား?" ဆိုတာကို ကန့်သတ်တယ်။
                SCP Allow ဆိုတာ:
                    SCP: Allow S3
                    ဒါကို: "Account ထဲက လူတိုင်း S3 သုံးလို့ရပြီ" လို့ မယူဆရပါဘူး။

                    IAM permission လည်း လိုသေးတယ်။
                        SCP
                        ↓
                        S3 allowed within the boundary
                            +
                        IAM
                        ↓
                        This user can use S3
                            ↓
                            ✅
                    ဒါကြောင့်: SCP alone ≠ permission
            Explicit Allow through the path:
                Must have an explicit allow from the root through each OU in the direct path to the target account
                        Root
                        │
                        │ Allow?
                        ▼
                        OU
                        │
                        │ Allow?
                        ▼
                        Account
                        │
                        │ IAM Allow?
                        ▼
                        Resource
                လမ်းတစ်လျှောက်မှာ applicable SCP policies တွေက action ကို ခွင့်ပြုထားရမယ်။
                တစ်နေရာမှာ restriction ရှိရင်:
                        Root
                        ↓
                        ✅
                        OU
                        ↓
                        ❌ DENY
                        ↓
                        Account
                    နောက်ဆုံး ❌ Access Denied
            Management Account exception:
                SCPs don't restrict permissions for users/roles in the Organization management account in the same way they restrict member accounts.
                Organization
                    │
                    ├── Management Account
                    │      └── SCPs don't restrict it
                    │
                    └── Member Accounts
                        └── SCPs can restrict them
            Note:
                Sandbox = စမ်းသပ်ဖို့နေရာ
                    စမ်းသပ်ကြည့်ဖို့ သီးသန့်ထားတဲ့ AWS Account တွေ
                Workloads = တကယ်အလုပ်လုပ်နေတဲ့ systems
                Sandbox vs Test:
                    Sandbox
                    → "Anything I want to experiment with"

                    Test
                    → "Test our actual application"
                    |             | Sandbox                   | Workloads          |
                    | ----------- | ------------------------- | ------------------ |
                    | ရည်ရွယ်ချက် | စမ်းသပ်ရန်                | တကယ်အသုံးပြုရန်    |
                    | Resources   | Experiment                | Real applications  |
                    | Risk        | ပိုလွတ်လပ်နိုင်           | ပိုတင်းကျပ်        |
                    | Example     | New AWS service စမ်းခြင်း | Company website    |
                    | Data        | Test data                 | Real/business data |
                    | SCP         | ပိုလွတ်လပ်စေနိုင်         | ပိုတင်းကျပ်စေနိုင် |

                | Topic                            | Remember                          |
                | -------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
                | Organization                     | Manage multiple AWS accounts      |
                | Management Account               | Main organization account         |
                | Member Account                   | Account inside organization       |
                | OU                               | Group of accounts                 |
                | Consolidated Billing             | Centralized billing               |
                | Aggregated usage                 | Potential volume pricing benefits |
                | Reserved Instances               | Discounts can be shared           |
                | Savings Plans                    | Discounts can be shared           |
                | CloudTrail                       | Can centralize audit logs         |
                | Cross-account access             | IAM `AssumeRole`                  |
                | SCP                              | **Restrict/guardrail**            |
                | SCP grants permission?           | ❌ No                              |
                | SCP denies/restricts?            | ✅ Yes                             |
                | SCP applies to member accounts?  | ✅                                 |
                | SCP restrict management account? | ❌                                 |
                | Multiple accounts                | Better isolation/governance       |

                |                                         | IAM Policy          | SCP                         |
                | --------------------------------------- | ------------------- | --------------------------- |
                | ဘယ်မှာ?                                 | User / Group / Role | Organization / OU / Account |
                | Permission ပေးလား?                      | ✅ Yes               | ❌ No                        |
                | Restrict လုပ်လား?                       | ✅ Can               | ✅ Yes                       |
                | Account-level guardrail                 | ❌                   | ✅                           |
                | User-specific                           | ✅                   | ❌                           |
                | Management account ကို SCP restriction? | —                   | ❌ Normal SCP model          |
                | Member accounts                         |                     | ✅                           |
            Tag Policies:
                A company-wide rule for how AWS resource tags should look.
                    Environment = Dev / Staging / Prod
                    Department  = IT / HR / Sales
                    Owner       = person's/team name
                    Project     = ProjectA / ProjectB
                Standardize tag keys and values:
                    example, your organization defines:
                        Tag key: Environment
                        Allowed values:
                            Dev
                            Staging
                            Prod
                        Then:
                            Environment = Dev       ✅
                            Environment = Staging   ✅
                            Environment = Prod      ✅

                            Environment = Production ❌
                            Environment = Test       ❌
                            env = Dev                ❌  (if standardized key is Environment)
                Where does Tag Policy live?
                    an AWS Organizations feature.
                    different OUs/accounts can apply  different tag requirements.
                Support tag compliance and can enforce tagging standards:
                Not automatically adding missing tags to every resource.
                Compliance report:
                    Generate a report that lists all tagged/non-compliant resources.
                        You can see which resources/accounts are compliant or non-compliant with the organization's tagging rules.
                                            Tag Policy
                                                │
                                    ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                                    ▼                     ▼
                                Account A              Account B
                                    │                     │
                                EC2 ❌                  S3 ✅
                                RDS ✅                  EC2 ❌
                                Lambda ✅              RDS ✅
                                    │                     │
                                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                                                ▼
                                        Compliance report
                EventBridge monitoring:
                    Use EventBridge to monitor non-compliant tags
                        eg:
                            Resource tagging
                                │
                                ▼
                            Tag Policy evaluation
                                │
                                ▼
                            Non-compliant
                                │
                                ▼
                            EventBridge
                                │
                            ┌───┴────┐
                            ▼        ▼
                            SNS       Lambda
                            Email     Automation
                        other:
                            EC2 tag changed
                                ↓
                            Tag Policy detects non-compliance
                                ↓
                            EventBridge
                                ↓
                            SNS
                                ↓
                            "EC2-123 has a non-compliant tag"
                Tag Policies + Cost Allocation Tags:
                    You can use tags for cost allocation.
                    Then your AWS costs can be categorized like:
                        AWS Cost
                        │
                        ├── Sales
                        │    ├── Website
                        │    └── CRM
                        │
                        ├── IT
                        │    ├── Monitoring
                        │    └── Internal Tools
                        │
                        └── HR
                            └── HR System
                    Tag Policy itself does not calculate your AWS bill.
                Tag Policies + ABAC:
                    ABAC = Attribute-Based Access Control.
                    Tags can become attributes used by IAM policies.
                        Tag Policy → consistent tags → easier ABAC.
                Tag Policy vs AWS Config vs SCP:
                    | Service                  | Question it answers                                |
                    | ------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------- |
                    | **Tag Policy**           | 🏷️ Are our tags standardized correctly?           |
                    | **AWS Config**           | 🔍 Is the resource configured/compliant correctly? |
                    | **SCP**                  | 🚧 What actions are accounts allowed to perform?   |
                    | **CloudTrail**           | 👤 Who performed the API action?                   |
                    | **Cost Allocation Tags** | 💰 How should costs be categorized?                |
    IAM Advanced Policy:
        aws:SourceIp:
            restrict the client IP frm API calls are being made.
            Deny access if the request does NOT come from one of these IP ranges.
                {
                    "Version": "2012-10-17",
                    "Statement": [
                        {
                            "Effect": "Deny",
                            "Action": "*",
                            "Resource": "*",
                            "Condition": {
                                "NotIpAddress": {
                                    "aws:SourceIp": [
                                        "192.0.2.0/24",
                                        "203.0.113.0/24"
                                    ]
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    ]
                }
        aws:RequestedRegion:
            restrict the region the API calls are make to.
            Deny EC2/RDS/DynamoDB requests when they are targeting those regions.
                {
                    "Version": "2012-10-17",
                    "Statement": [
                        {
                            "Effect": "Deny",
                                "Action": [
                                    "ec2:*",
                                    "rds:*",
                                    "dynamodb:*"
                                ],
                                "Resource": "*",
                                "Condition": {
                                "StringEquals": {
                                    "aws:RequestedRegion": [
                                    "us-central-1",
                                    "eu-west-1"
                                    ]
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    ]
                }
        ec2:ResourceTag:
            Check the tags on the EC2 resource, then decide whether the action is allowed or denied.
            Developers can manage Dev instances, but cannot manage Prod instances.
                {
                    "Version": "2012-10-17",
                    "Statement": [
                        {
                            "Effect": "Allow",
                            "Action": [
                                "ec2:StartInstances",
                                "ec2:StopInstances",
                                "ec2:RebootInstances"
                            ],
                            "Resource": "*",
                            "Condition": {
                                "StringEquals": {
                                    "ec2:ResourceTag/Environment": "Dev"
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    ]
                }
        ResourceTag vs RequestTag:
                ResourceTag
                    ↓
                "What tag does the resource ALREADY have?"

                RequestTag
                    ↓
                "What tag is being supplied IN THIS REQUEST?"
            🧠 Resource = existing object
            🧠 Request = incoming operation
            eg: 
                Company rule: Developers can create and manage only Dev EC2 instances.

                Creation
                    Require: aws:RequestTag/Environment = Dev
                Existing resources
                    Require: :ResourceTag/Environment = Dev
                                    Developer
                                        │
                            ┌─────────┴─────────┐
                            │                   │
                        Create EC2           Manage EC2
                            │                   │
                            ▼                   ▼
                    RequestTag             ResourceTag
                            │                   │
                    Environment=Dev       Environment=Dev
                            │                   │
                            ▼                   ▼
                            ✅                  ✅
        aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent:
            Allow this action only when the request was authenticated with MFA.
                {
                    "Version": "2012-10-17",
                    "Statement": [
                        {
                            "Effect": "Deny",
                            "Action": "*",
                            "Resource": "*",
                            "Condition": {
                                "BoolIfExists": {
                                    "aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent": "false"
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    ]
                }
        S3 Bucket and Object Level Permission:
            S3 Bucket
                │
                ├── Bucket-level permissions
                │      └── ListBucket
                │
                └── Object-level permissions
                        ├── GetObject
                        ├── PutObject
                        ├── DeleteObject
                        └── ...
            s3:ListBucket → bucket-level
            s3:GetObject / PutObject / DeleteObject → object-level
            s3:ListBucket does NOT mean: “Can I download those objects?”, read/download is s3:GetObject.
            Important:
                eg: you want a user to:
                    List the bucket and download files from documents/, but not upload or delete anything.
                        {
                            "Version": "2012-10-17",
                            "Statement": [
                                {
                                    "Effect": "Allow",
                                    "Action": "s3:ListBucket",
                                    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket"
                                },
                                {
                                    "Effect": "Allow",
                                    "Action": "s3:GetObject",
                                    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket/documents/*"
                                }
                            ]
                        }
                    Why can't I use the same ARN?
                        You might think:
                            {
                                "Effect": "Allow",
                                "Action": [
                                    "s3:ListBucket",
                                    "s3:GetObject"
                                ],
                                "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket/*"
                            }
                            ❌ That's wrong for ListBucket.

                            Why?
                            Because:
                                Bucket ARN:
                                arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket

                                Object ARN:
                                arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket/*
                            Different levels.
                        Level Actions:
                            | Action                   | Meaning                        | Level     |
                            | ------------------------ | ------------------------------ | --------- |
                            | `s3:ListBucket`          | List objects in bucket         | 🪣 Bucket |
                            | `s3:GetObject`           | Read/download object           | 📄 Object |
                            | `s3:PutObject`           | Upload/create object           | 📄 Object |
                            | `s3:DeleteObject`        | Delete object                  | 📄 Object |
                            | `s3:GetObjectVersion`    | Read a specific object version | 📄 Object |
                            | `s3:DeleteObjectVersion` | Delete a specific version      | 📄 Object |
                            | `s3:GetBucketLocation`   | Get bucket Region              | 🪣 Bucket |
        aws:PrincipalOrgID:
            aws:PrincipalOrgID lets a resource policy trust principals that belong to a particular AWS Organization.
            especially useful for cross-account access.
            Principal  = Who is allowed to access the resource?
                "Principal": {
                    "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:root"
                }
                means that AWS account is the principal being trusted
                if we have 50 accounts, aws:PrincipalOrgID makes this much easier.
                aws:PrincipalOrgID:
                    What AWS Organization does the requesting principal belong to?
                    "Condition": {
                        "StringEquals": {
                            "aws:PrincipalOrgID": "o-abcd1234"
                        }
                    }
                full sample:
                    {
                        "Version": "2012-10-17",
                        "Statement": [
                            {
                                "Effect": "Allow",
                                "Principal": "*",
                                "Action": [
                                    "s3:GetObject"
                                ],
                                "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::company-shared-data/*",
                                "Condition": {
                                    "StringEquals": {
                                        "aws:PrincipalOrgID": "o-abcd1234"
                                    }
                                }
                            }
                        ]
                    }
                    At first, this may look scary:
                        "Principal": "*"
                    because it looks like:
                        “Everyone can access my bucket!”
                        But the Condition restricts it:
                            Principal = *
                                │
                                ▼
                            Is PrincipalOrgID = o-abcd1234?
                                │
                            ┌───┴────┐
                            YES       NO
                            │         │
                            ▼         ▼
                            ✅         ❌
                PrincipalOrgID vs Principal:
                    Principal => WHO exactly?
                    PrincipalOrgID => WHICH ORGANIZATION
                Note:
                    | Condition                    | Checks                                              |
                    | ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- |
                    | `aws:SourceIp`               | 🌐 Where is request coming from?                    |
                    | `aws:RequestedRegion`        | 🌎 Which AWS Region is targeted?                    |
                    | `aws:PrincipalOrgID`         | 🏢 Which AWS Organization does principal belong to? |
                    | `aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent` | 🔐 Was MFA used?                                    |
                    | `ec2:ResourceTag/...`        | 🏷️ What tag does the EC2 resource have?            |
        IAM Roles vs Resource-Based:
            question: “Should I give the user a role to access another account, or should the resource itself trust the user/account?”
            eg: Account A wants to access the S3 bucket in Account B.
            Two ways to do cross-account access:
                Resource-based policy:
                    Account A User
                        │
                        │ access
                        ▼
                    Account B S3 Bucket
                        │
                        └── Bucket Policy
                            ↓
                        Trust Account A ("Account A ကို ယုံတယ်")
                    Put a policy directly on the S3 bucket.
                    like:
                        {
                            "Version": "2012-10-17",
                            "Statement": [
                                {
                                    "Effect": "Allow",
                                    "Principal": {
                                        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111111111111:root"
                                    },
                                    "Action": "s3:GetObject",
                                    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::company-data/*"
                                }
                            ]
                        }
                    The S3 bucket trusts Account A to read objects.
                    Account A ရဲ့ original identity က မပြောင်းဘူး။
                IAM Role:
                    Account A User
                        │
                        │ AssumeRole
                        ▼
                    Account B IAM Role
                        │
                        ▼
                    S3 Bucket
                    The role acts as a proxy.
                    like:
                        Account B
                            └── IAM Role: ReadCompanyData

                            The role has:
                            {
                                "Effect": "Allow",
                                "Action": "s3:GetObject",
                                "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::company-data/*"
                            }
                        The user temporarily becomes the role's identity.
                    Account A က Account B က Role ကို Assume လုပ်တယ်။
                    Account B က Role ရဲ့ temporary credentials ကိုသုံးပြီး S3 access လုပ်နေတယ်။
                    Give up original permissions:
                        Role Assume လုပ်လိုက်ရင် Role ရဲ့ identity/permissions နဲ့ အလုပ်လုပ်တာ
                Example: DynamoDB -> S3:
                    Requirement: Account A ထဲက User က Account A ရဲ့ DynamoDB Table ကို Scan လုပ်ပြီး Account B ရဲ့ S3 Bucket ထဲကို data dump လုပ်ချင်တယ်
                                    Account A
                                ┌─────────────────────┐
                                │                     │
                                │ Alice               │
                                │   │                 │
                                │   │ DynamoDB Scan   │
                                │   ▼                 │
                                │ DynamoDB Table      │
                                │                     │
                                └─────────────────────┘
                                        │
                                        │ S3 PutObject
                                        ▼
                                ┌─────────────────────┐
                                │     Account B       │
                                │                     │
                                │     S3 Bucket       │
                                │     company-data    │
                                │                     │
                                └─────────────────────┘
                        Alice လိုအပ်တာက—
                            1. DynamoDB Scan permission
                            2. S3 PutObject permission
                        Resource-Based Policy နဲ့လုပ်ရင်:
                            Account B ရဲ့ S3 Bucket Policy က Account A ကို trust လုပ်တယ်
                            Principal ပါတယ်
                        Role နဲ့လုပ်ရင်
                            Role ရဲ့ temporary credentials သုံးနေတယ်
                            Role မှာ Policy ၂ ခုရှိတာကို မမေ့နဲ့:
                                Trust Policy:
                                    ဘယ်သူက ဒီ Role ကို Assume လုပ်နိုင်လဲ?
                                Permission Policy:
                                    Role ကို Assume ပြီးရင် ဘာလုပ်နိုင်လဲ?
                Question Match:
                    Allow Account A to access an S3 bucket in Account B. => Resource-based Policy
                    Users from Account A must assume a role in Account B  => IAM Role
                    Users need temporary credentials to access resources => IAM Role
                    Grant access without requiring the user to assume a role  => Resource-based Policy

                    sample:
                        ကုမ္ပဏီ Building တစ်ခုရှိတယ်လို့ယူပါ။
                        Resource-Based Policy
                            Building owner ကပြောတယ်— "ဒီလူကို ဝင်ခွင့်ပေးတယ်။"
                        IAM Role
                            Building owner ကပြောတယ်— "ဒီ access card ကို ခဏယူသုံးပါ။ ဒီ card မှာ သတ်မှတ်ထားတဲ့ access ပဲရမယ်။"
            Amazon Eventbridge - Security:
                Sometimes EventBridge uses a Resource-Based Policy, and sometimes it uses an IAM Role.
                Note:
                    | EventBridge Target          | Permission mechanism     |
                    | --------------------------- | ------------------------ |
                    | Lambda                      | 🟢 Resource-based policy |
                    | SNS                         | 🟢 Resource-based policy |
                    | SQS                         | 🟢 Resource-based policy |
                    | S3 bucket                   | 🟢 Resource-based policy |
                    | API Gateway                 | 🟢 Resource-based policy |
                    | Kinesis Data Stream         | 🔵 IAM Role              |
                    | EC2 Auto Scaling            | 🔵 IAM Role              |
                    | Systems Manager Run Command | 🔵 IAM Role              |
                    | ECS Task                    | 🔵 IAM Role              |
        IAM Permission Boundaries:
            maximum permissions an IAM user or role can ever receive.(for user and role not group)
            a limit, not grant permission.
            IAM Policy:
                grants permission
            example:
                Suppose Bob has this IAM policy:
                        {
                            "Effect": "Allow",
                            "Action": [
                                "s3:*",
                                "ec2:*"
                            ],
                            "Resource": "*"
                        }
                    Without a boundary:
                        Bob
                        ↓
                        S3:*  ✅
                        EC2:* ✅
                    Now attach a boundary:

                        {
                            "Effect": "Allow",
                            "Action": [
                                "s3:*"
                            ],
                            "Resource": "*"
                        }
                    Now:
                        IAM Policy says: S3:* + EC2:*

                        Boundary says: S3:*

                        Effective:
                            S3:*   ✅
                            EC2:*  ❌
                analogy:
                    Imagine you work for a company.
                        Your manager says: "You can potentially do many things."

                        That's your IAM Policy.
                        But the company gives you an employee access card:
                            "This card physically allows you to enter only these rooms."
                        That's the Permission Boundary.
                        Even if your manager says:
                            "You can enter every room."
                        your access card still limits you.
                                IAM Policy
                                ↓
                                "What permissions are granted?"

                                Permission Boundary
                                ↓
                                "What is the maximum allowed?"
                                            ↓
                                    Effective permissions
                    Why would a company use Permission Boundaries?
                        The most common use case is: 👨‍💻 Delegated administration

                        Imagine:
                            Company
                            │
                            ├── Security Team
                            │
                            ├── Platform Team
                            │
                            └── Developers

                        The Platform Team should be allowed to create IAM roles for developers.
                        But you don't want Platform Team members to create an unrestricted administrator role.
                        So you define:
                            Developer Role
                                │
                                ▼
                            Permission Boundary
                                │
                                ├── S3
                                ├── DynamoDB
                                ├── Lambda
                                └── CloudWatch
                                
                                ❌ IAM
                                ❌ Organizations
                        Now Platform Team can create roles, but those roles can't exceed the organization's security boundary.
            use cases:
                Delegate responsibilities to non-administrators:
                    problem: "I want someone to manage IAM permissions, but I don't want them to become Administrator."
                    Imagine your company has:
                        Management/Admin
                            │
                            ├── Platform Team
                            ├── Developer Team
                            └── Data Team
                        The Admin doesn't want to create every IAM user manually.
                        Instead, Admin says:
                            "Platform Team, you can create IAM users, but those users must stay inside this Permission Boundary."
                        For example:
                            Permission Boundary
                            ─────────────────────
                            S3
                            Lambda
                            CloudWatch
                            DynamoDB

                            ❌ IAM administration
                            ❌ Organizations
                            ❌ Billing

                        Now Platform Team can create:

                            User: DeveloperA
                            │
                            ├── IAM Policy
                            │     └── S3 + Lambda
                            │
                            └── Permission Boundary
                                    └── S3 + Lambda + CloudWatch
                        Even if Platform Team accidentally gives DeveloperA:
                            AdministratorAccess
                        DeveloperA still cannot exceed the boundary.
                Developers self-manage their permissions:
                    problem:
                        This one sounds scary at first:
                            "Allow developers to self-assign policies."
                            You might think:
                                "Wait! If they can assign policies, can't they give themselves AdministratorAccess?"
                            Normally, yes, that could be a privilege-escalation problem.
                            Privilege escalation(အခွင့်အရေးအဆင့်မြှင့်တင်ခြင်း) means:
                                A user finds a way to obtain permissions greater than
                                the permissions they were supposed to have.
                                အသုံးပြုသူတစ်ဦးသည် မိမိရရှိထားသင့်သည့် အခွင့်အရေးများထက် ပိုမိုမြင့်မားသော အခွင့်အရေးများကို ရယူနိုင်မည့်နည်းလမ်းကို ရှာဖွေတွေ့ရှိခြင်း ဖြစ်သည်။
                Restrict ONE specific user:
                    Suppose you have:
                            AWS Organization
                            │
                            ├── Account A
                            │    ├── Alice
                            │    ├── Bob
                            │    └── Charlie
                            │
                            └── Account B
                                ├── David
                                └── Eve
                        You discover:
                            "Alice needs special restrictions."
                        But Bob and Charlie are fine.
                        You don't want to restrict the entire AWS Account.
                        So: Permission Boundary
                        Attach boundary only to Alice:
                            Alice
                            │
                            └── Permission Boundary
                                    ↓
                                Limited

                        Bob and Charlie:
                            Bob/Charlie
                            │
                            └── Normal permissions
                        Only Alice is restricted.
                        Compare with SCP:
                            This is the easiest way to understand the difference.
                            Permission Boundary
                                Account
                                │
                                ├── Alice ← 🚧 Boundary
                                ├── Bob
                                └── Charlie
                        One IAM user/role can be restricted.
                            SCP
                            AWS Account
                            │
                            ├── Alice    ❌
                            ├── Bob      ❌
                            └── Charlie  ❌
                        An SCP is an account/OU-level guardrail, so it affects principals in that scope.
                        Therefore:
                            Need to restrict one particular IAM user/role → Permission Boundary
                            Need to restrict an entire AWS account/OU → SCP
            Permission Boundary vs SCP:
                SCP = organization/account-level guardrail
                Permission Boundary = user/role-level guardrail
                |                                   | SCP                      | Permission Boundary     |
                | --------------------------------- | ------------------------ | ----------------------- |
                | Scope                             | Account / OU             | IAM user / role         |
                | Managed by                        | AWS Organizations        | IAM                     |
                | Purpose                           | Account-level guardrail  | User/role-level maximum |
                | Grants permissions?               | ❌                        | ❌                       |
                | Limits permissions?               | ✅                        | ✅                       |
                | Can prevent privilege escalation? | ✅                        | ✅                       |
                | Applies to management account?    | SCP has special behavior | Not relevant            |
            Permission Boundary vs IAM Policy:
                    IAM Policy
                        ↓
                    GRANTS permissions

                    Permission Boundary
                        ↓
                    LIMITS maximum permissions
                For effective permissions, think roughly:
                    Identity-based permissions
                            ∩
                    Permission Boundary
                            ↓
                    Effective permissions
                Example:
                    IAM Policy:
                    S3 + EC2 + DynamoDB

                    Boundary:
                    S3 + DynamoDB

                    Effective:
                    S3 + DynamoDB
            Permission Boundary does not override everything:
                Permission Boundary is not a magical "allow."
                think: "If this user gets permissions, they cannot exceed this boundary."
                    So:
                        IAM Policy says:
                            Allow EC2
                        Boundary says:
                            EC2 NOT allowed
                        Result:
                            ❌ EC2

                        And:
                            IAM Policy says:
                                ❌ No S3 permission
                            Boundary says:
                                ✅ S3 allowed
                            Result:
                                ❌ S3
            Policy Evaluation Logic:
                Explicit Deny ရှိရင် Allow အားလုံးကို override လုပ်တယ်။
                Deny evaluation -> Organization SCPs -> Resource-based policies -> Identity-based Policies -> IAM permissions boundaries -> Session policies.
            keynote:
                If you see:
                    "Set the maximum permissions that an IAM user or role can have."
                    🎯 Permission Boundary
                If you see:
                    "Prevent developers from escalating privileges when creating IAM roles."
                    🎯 Permission Boundary
                If you see:
                    "Limit what an AWS account can do across an Organization."
                    🎯 SCP
                If you see
                    "Give a user permission to access S3."
                    🎯 IAM Policy
    IAM Identity Center:
        လူတွေကို AWS Organizations ရဲ့ account အများကြီးထဲကို centralized login + permission ပေးတဲ့ service
        ဘာကြောင့် IAM Identity Center လိုတာလဲ?
            ဥပမာ company မှာ AWS accounts 3 ခုရှိတယ်
                AWS Organization
                │
                ├── Development Account
                ├── Staging Account
                └── Production Account
            Developer 10 ယောက်ရှိရင် account တစ်ခုချင်းစီမှာ IAM User 10 ယောက်လုပ်မယ်ဆိုရင်—
                Dev Account       → 10 IAM Users
                Staging Account   → 10 IAM Users
                Production        → 10 IAM Users
                                        ↓
                                    30 IAM Users 😵
            Password / MFA / permissions management တွေလည်း ရှုပ်လာမယ်။
            IAM Identity Center သုံးရင်
                                    IAM Identity Center
                                        │
                                    Central Login
                                        │
                        ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
                        ↓                ↓                ↓
                    Dev Account     Staging Account   Prod Account
                        │                │                │
                    Permission       Permission       Permission
                        Set              Set              Set
            User က တစ်နေရာတည်း login လုပ်ပြီး သူ့မှာ permission ရှိတဲ့ AWS accounts တွေကို ဝင်နိုင်တယ်။
            One Login:
                successor to AWS Single Sign-On (AWS SSO):
                    AWS SSO ရဲ့ newer/rebranded service
                    One login for AWS accounts
                        One login → many AWS accounts + many business applications
                Business cloud applications:
                    AWS account တွေအပြင် business applications တွေကိုလည်း Single Sign-On လုပ်နိုင်တယ်။
                        ဥပမာ—
                                            IAM Identity Center
                                                │
                                ┌────────────────┼─────────────────┐
                                ↓                ↓                 ↓
                                AWS             Salesforce          Box
                            Accounts
                                                │
                                                Microsoft 365
                        ဒါကြောင့် employee က application တစ်ခုချင်းစီအတွက် username/password ထပ်ထည့်စရာ မလိုအောင် centralized authentication လုပ်နိုင်တယ်။
                SAML 2.0-enabled applications:
                    SAML 2.0 က authentication / SSO အတွက် standard protocol တစ်ခုပါ။
                        ဥပမာ company မှာ—
                                IAM Identity Center
                                        │
                                        │ SAML 2.0
                                        ↓
                                Company Application
                        Application က SAML 2.0 support လုပ်ရင် IAM Identity Center ကနေ SSO လုပ်နိုင်တယ်။
                EC2 Windows Instances:
                    IAM Identity Center ကို EC2 Windows Server တွေမှာ user login အတွက်လည်း အသုံးပြုနိုင်
                        Employee
                            ↓
                            IAM Identity Center
                            ↓
                            EC2 Windows Instance
                            ↓
                            Windows Login
                Identity Providers:
                    IAM Identity Center မှာ user တွေ ဘယ်ကလာသလဲဆိုတာကို Identity Provider (IdP) က သတ်မှတ်ပေးတယ်။
        IAM User နဲ့ မတူဘူး
            ဒီ distinction က SAA exam မှာ အရေးကြီးတယ်။
                Traditional IAM:
                    IAM User
                    ↓
                    IAM Policy
                    ↓
                    One AWS Account
                IAM Identity Center:
                    User
                    ↓
                    IAM Identity Center
                    ↓
                    AWS Account
                    ↓
                    Permission Set
                    ↓
                    IAM Role
                အထူးသဖြင့်—
                    Permission Set ကို အရေးကြီးဆုံး keyword အနေနဲ့ မှတ်ပါ။
                    Multi-Account Permissions:
                        AWS Organizations ထဲမှာ account အများကြီးရှိရင် account တစ်ခုချင်းစီကို access manage လုပ်နိုင်
        Permission Set = User/Group တစ်ယောက်က AWS Account ထဲမှာ
            ဘာလုပ်ခွင့်ရှိမလဲဆိုတာ သတ်မှတ်ထားတဲ့ permission template
            eg:
                Permission Sets
                    ReadOnly
                    Developer
                    DatabaseAdmin
                    Administrator
        User တစ်ယောက်ကို Account + Permission Set assign လုပ်တာ
                User
                │
                ↓
                Group
                │
                ↓
                Permission Set
                │
                ↓
                AWS Account
            eg:
                Alice
                ↓
                Developers Group
                ↓
                Developer Permission Set
                ↓
                Development Account
                Alice က Development account ထဲ login ဝင်တဲ့အခါ သူ့အတွက် permission set အပေါ်အခြေခံပြီး IAM role တစ်ခုကို AWS က အသုံးပြုစေတယ်။
        External Identity Provider (IdP):
            AWS ထဲက user database တစ်ခုတည်းနဲ့ပဲ သုံးရမယ်လို့ မဟုတ်
            Company မှာ existing identity system ရှိရင် ချိတ်နိုင်တယ်။
                Microsoft Entra ID
                    │
                    ↓
                IAM Identity Center
                    │
                    ↓
                AWS Accounts
            keyword:
                “Employees already use an existing corporate identity provider”
                → IAM Identity Center + external IdP
        IAM Identity Center vs IAM Role:
            IAM Role
                AWS resource/service access အတွက် temporary permissions ပေးတဲ့ mechanism
            IAM Identity Center
                Human users တွေကို AWS accounts အများကြီးအတွက် centralized access ပေးတဲ့ service
                eg:
                    Human employee
                        ↓
                    IAM Identity Center
                        ↓
                    AWS Account
                        ↓
                    IAM Role
                    user က temporary role session နဲ့ AWS resources တွေကို access လုပ်
            | IAM User                              | IAM Identity Center                      |
            | ------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
            | Account-level identity                | Centralized identity/access              |
            | Account တစ်ခုချင်း manage လုပ်ရတာများ | Multiple AWS accounts ကို central manage |
            | Long-term credentials possible        | Temporary role-based access              |
            | Small/simple environments             | Multi-account organizations              |
            | IAM users                             | Users + Groups + Permission Sets         |
